========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.

And it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.

It’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… anxious is probably the right word. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.

I’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.

And the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.

I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.

The retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.

And even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.

But hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.

I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.

I still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.

If you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n7\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0, F32.1\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.\n\nI’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.

But it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.

And the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.

I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.

My mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.

Even when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.\n\nI’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the time, even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nEven when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.

If I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.

And then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. But that doesn’t seem to stop my mind from circling back to it.

So… no, not completely worry-free. There might be moments where I’m distracted or absorbed in something, but whole areas of life where I feel totally free from worry? Not really.

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.\n\nI’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the time, even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nEven when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.\n\nAnd then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. But that doesn’t seem to stop my mind from circling back to it.\n\nSo… no, not completely worry-free. There might be moments where I’m distracted or absorbed in something, but whole areas of life where I feel totally free from worry? Not really.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yes, I think it is. I might have been reluctant to say that at first, because I can still function, you know — I still show up, I still get things done eventually. But it’s definitely interfering.

The biggest place I notice it is with the retirement handoff and the clinic. I’ve had to slow parts of it down, or postpone decisions, because I keep going back over things I thought were settled. I’ll look at some part of the arrangement and think, “Did I handle that correctly? Was that the right call?” And then instead of moving forward, I lose time at the clinic circling around it. It’s not always visible to other people, maybe, but it eats up a lot of mental space and time.

And even outside of work, things I used to do to feel steadier don’t feel as easy. Painting, gardening — those were always manageable for me, almost grounding. Now I find myself having trouble settling into them, or I start and then get pulled back into worrying about the handoff or what still needs to be done. So yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit.

I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the time, even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nEven when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.\n\nAnd then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. But that doesn’t seem to stop my mind from circling back to it.\n\nSo… no, not completely worry-free. There might be moments where I’m distracted or absorbed in something, but whole areas of life where I feel totally free from worry? Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I might have been reluctant to say that at first, because I can still function, you know — I still show up, I still get things done eventually. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with the retirement handoff and the clinic. I’ve had to slow parts of it down, or postpone decisions, because I keep going back over things I thought were settled. I’ll look at some part of the arrangement and think, “Did I handle that correctly? Was that the right call?” And then instead of moving forward, I lose time at the clinic circling around it. It’s not always visible to other people, maybe, but it eats up a lot of mental space and time.\n\nAnd even outside of work, things I used to do to feel steadier don’t feel as easy. Painting, gardening — those were always manageable for me, almost grounding. Now I find myself having trouble settling into them, or I start and then get pulled back into worrying about the handoff or what still needs to be done. So yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Restless or on edge… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t necessarily describe myself as pacing around the house all day or visibly agitated, if that’s what you mean.

But I suppose there are times when I don’t feel quite settled. It’s more of an internal thing than something obvious from the outside. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should be doing something, checking something, resolving something — even if there isn’t actually a clear task in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call that “restless,” exactly.

“On edge” maybe fits a little better, but again, I’m not sure. I’m not jumping at every sound or anything like that. It’s more that I have a hard time feeling fully at ease. Like part of me is braced for something I’ve overlooked. So… yes, maybe somewhat, but I don’t know how often I’d say. It’s not the word I would have used first, but it may be there underneath.

  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n13\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF41.1, F41.0\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. At least, not that I’ve noticed in a clear way.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious lately?\n\nPatient: Yes… anxious is probably the right word. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.\n\nI’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the time, even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nEven when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.\n\nAnd then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. But that doesn’t seem to stop my mind from circling back to it.\n\nSo… no, not completely worry-free. There might be moments where I’m distracted or absorbed in something, but whole areas of life where I feel totally free from worry? Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I might have been reluctant to say that at first, because I can still function, you know — I still show up, I still get things done eventually. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with the retirement handoff and the clinic. I’ve had to slow parts of it down, or postpone decisions, because I keep going back over things I thought were settled. I’ll look at some part of the arrangement and think, “Did I handle that correctly? Was that the right call?” And then instead of moving forward, I lose time at the clinic circling around it. It’s not always visible to other people, maybe, but it eats up a lot of mental space and time.\n\nAnd even outside of work, things I used to do to feel steadier don’t feel as easy. Painting, gardening — those were always manageable for me, almost grounding. Now I find myself having trouble settling into them, or I start and then get pulled back into worrying about the handoff or what still needs to be done. morning doesn’t fix, so even simple things like weeding the garden or standing at the easel feel heavier than they used to.\n- key: S056\n  name: Excessive_Anxiety_Worry\n  description: Excessive patient, but one concern just leads to the next until I’m checking emails, reviewing notes, or asking the same questions again instead of letting it go.\n- key: S059\n  name: functional_impairment\n  name: Functional Impairment\n  description: required\n  manifestation: I’ve had to slow down and postpone parts of the retirement handoff because I keep second-guessing decisions and losing time at the clinic, and even my painting and gardening don’t feel as manageable as they used to.\n- key: duration\n  name: Duration\n  description: 1 year and 3 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since I started the formal retirement process about a year and three months ago.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 64 / dentist / bachelors\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S059\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.\n\nAnd then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. I’ve had to slow parts of it down, or postpone decisions, because I keep going back over things I thought were settled. It’s not always visible to other people, maybe, but it eats up a lot of mental space and time.\n\nAnd even outside of work, things I used to do to feel steadier don’t feel as easy. Painting, gardening — those were always manageable for me, almost grounding. Now I find myself having trouble settling into them, or I start and then get pulled back into worrying about the handoff or what still needs to be done. So yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless or on edge… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t necessarily describe myself as pacing around the house all day or visibly agitated, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I suppose there are times when I don’t feel quite settled. It’s more of an internal thing than something obvious from the outside. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should be doing something, checking something, resolving something — even if there isn’t actually a clear task in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call that “restless,” exactly.\n\n“On edge” maybe fits a little better, but again, I’m not sure. I’m not jumping at every sound or anything like that. It’s more that I have a hard time feeling fully at ease. Like part of me is braced for something I’ve overlooked. So… yes, maybe somewhat, but I don’t know how often I’d say. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
No, I don’t think there are really areas where it’s completely absent. I wish I could say there were, but it tends to attach itself to whatever feels important at the time.

Like, if I’m thinking about my former patients, my mind starts turning over whether they’re being cared for properly, whether they feel I stepped away too abruptly, whether I did enough to make sure they were in good hands. And then if I try to reassure myself about that, it shifts to the practice handoff — whether the financial side was sound, whether I missed something, whether I made a decision that’s going to come back to haunt me somehow.

And then there’s this larger thing about retirement itself. I keep worrying that once I’m fully retired I’ll become… I don’t know, useless, or irrelevant. Saying it out loud, I can hear that it’s probably too much. It feels out of proportion even to me. But knowing that doesn’t really make it disappear.

So I wouldn’t say the worry is completely absent anywhere. It quiets down sometimes, maybe, but it doesn’t feel gone.

I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. But I wouldn’t say I’ve been walking around feeling persistently sad. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. It’s more that my mind gets caught in these loops and I can’t seem to set them down.\n\nI’ll start thinking about the patients I used to see regularly—some of them for years—and I’ll wonder whether they’re really being looked after properly now, even though rationally I know there are systems in place. Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly.
