Then I’ll move from that to whether the way I handed off parts of the practice was financially sound, whether I missed something, whether I should have negotiated differently, and on and on. And then somehow it becomes this bigger question of what I’m going to be worth once I’m fully retired, whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the thing is, part of me can see that the level of worry is out of proportion. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. It just keeps circling.\n\nDoctor: Have you experienced any episodes of intense fear or discomfort that reach a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in the way you’re describing, where it sort of surges up and peaks within a few minutes. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. I can feel very uncomfortable and keyed up, but I’m not sure I’ve had distinct episodes where I’d say, “Oh, that was a sudden attack of fear that peaked and then passed.” If that’s what you mean, then no, I haven’t really noticed that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been avoiding certain situations or activities because of your worries?\n\nPatient: Yes… I suppose I have, though I don’t always think of it as “avoiding” in the obvious sense. It’s not like I’m refusing to leave the house or anything like that. But I’ve definitely slowed down on things I was supposed to be doing.\n\nThe retirement handoff, for example — there are parts of it I’ve postponed or dragged out because every time I’m about to make a decision, I start second-guessing whether it’s the right one. I’ll sit with paperwork or plans at the clinic and lose a ridiculous amount of time going back over things I’ve already gone over. Then I’ll tell myself I need to think about it more before moving forward, which sounds reasonable in the moment, but really it means I’m delaying.\n\nAnd even outside the clinic, things I used to do to relax don’t feel as simple. Painting, gardening — they used to be manageable, almost automatic in a nice way. Now I’ll put them off or start and then get caught up worrying about what else I should be handling. So yes, I think my worries have made me step back from certain activities, or at least made it much harder to keep doing them the way I used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling hopeless about your future?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it hopeless, exactly. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. I’ll start by thinking about some patient I treated for years and wondering if they’re okay with the new arrangement, and then somehow I’m thinking about whether I made the right financial decisions, and then whether I’ll have any purpose once I’m fully retired, or whether I’ll just become… I don’t know, useless.\n\nAnd the frustrating thing is that part of me can see it’s probably out of proportion. I’m not completely unaware of that. But knowing that doesn’t seem to make it stop. It just keeps moving from one worry to the next.\n\nDoctor: Do you find that your worries are present most of the time, even when you're not actively trying to think about them?\n\nPatient: Yes… yes, I think that’s a fair way to put it. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. And I know that sounds a bit dramatic when I say it out loud, because part of me can see that the level of worry is probably more than the situation calls for. But seeing that doesn’t really make it go away.\n\nEven when I’m trying not to think about it, it’s like it’s waiting for me. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. Then if I try to tell myself, “Well, that’s mostly out of your hands now,” my mind just shifts over to the practice handoff — whether it was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether I should have done something differently.\n\nAnd then underneath all of that there’s this bigger worry about what happens to me once I’m fully retired. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. But that doesn’t seem to stop my mind from circling back to it.\n\nSo… no, not completely worry-free. There might be moments where I’m distracted or absorbed in something, but whole areas of life where I feel totally free from worry? Not really.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like your worries are interfering with your daily life or relationships?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it is. I might have been reluctant to say that at first, because I can still function, you know — I still show up, I still get things done eventually. But it’s definitely interfering.\n\nThe biggest place I notice it is with the retirement handoff and the clinic. I’ve had to slow parts of it down, or postpone decisions, because I keep going back over things I thought were settled. I’ll look at some part of the arrangement and think, “Did I handle that correctly? Was that the right call?” And then instead of moving forward, I lose time at the clinic circling around it. It’s not always visible to other people, maybe, but it eats up a lot of mental space and time.\n\nAnd even outside of work, things I used to do to feel steadier don’t feel as easy. Painting, gardening — those were always manageable for me, almost grounding. Now I find myself having trouble settling into them, or I start and then get pulled back into worrying about the handoff or what still needs to be done. So yes, it’s affecting my day-to-day life more than I’d like to admit.\n\nDoctor: Do you often feel restless or on edge?\n\nPatient: Restless or on edge… I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t necessarily describe myself as pacing around the house all day or visibly agitated, if that’s what you mean.\n\nBut I suppose there are times when I don’t feel quite settled. It’s more of an internal thing than something obvious from the outside. I’ll be sitting there and feel like I should be doing something, checking something, resolving something — even if there isn’t actually a clear task in front of me. But I don’t know if I’d call that “restless,” exactly.\n\n“On edge” maybe fits a little better, but again, I’m not sure. I’m not jumping at every sound or anything like that. It’s more that I have a hard time feeling fully at ease. Like part of me is braced for something I’ve overlooked. So… yes, maybe somewhat, but I don’t know how often I’d say. It’s not the word I would have used first, but it may be there underneath.\n\nDoctor: Are there any aspects of your life where you feel your anxiety or worries are completely absent?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think there are really areas where it’s completely absent. I wish I could say there were, but it tends to attach itself to whatever feels important at the time.\n\nLike, if I’m thinking about my former patients, my mind starts turning over whether they’re being cared for properly, whether they feel I stepped away too abruptly, whether I did enough to make sure they were in good hands. And then if I try to reassure myself about that, it shifts to the practice handoff — whether the financial side was sound, whether I missed something, whether I made a decision that’s going to come back to haunt me somehow.\n\nAnd then there’s this larger thing about retirement itself. I keep worrying that once I’m fully retired I’ll become… I don’t know, useless, or irrelevant. Saying it out loud, I can hear that it’s probably too much. It feels out of proportion even to me. But knowing that doesn’t really make it disappear.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the worry is completely absent anywhere. It quiets down sometimes, maybe, but it doesn’t feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or set of situations that triggers your anxiety, or does it feel more constant and unrelated to specific events?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. There are definitely situations that set it off more strongly — anything connected to the practice handoff, for example. If I get an email or have to look over some detail about the financial side, my mind can latch onto it and just keep going: Did I miss something? Was that arrangement really sound? Should I have handled it differently?\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly contained to that. It sort of spreads. I’ll start with the practice, and then I’m thinking about my old patients — whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left things in a way that was fair to them, whether someone is going to fall through the cracks because I’m not there watching over things the way I used to.\n\nAnd then it goes even wider than that, into retirement itself. I’ll catch myself worrying that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant. I know that sounds dramatic, and honestly it feels out of proportion even to me when I say it. But in the moment, it doesn’t feel easy to just dismiss.\n\nSo yes, certain things trigger it, but it also feels more constant than I’d like. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even when one concern quiets down, another one seems to take its place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about one or two main things, or is it about a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more than one or two things. I mean, there are a few main themes that I seem to come back to, but they branch out pretty quickly.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether I left them in good hands, whether something important is going to be missed now that I’m not the one overseeing it. Then it shifts to the practice handoff — the financial side, whether it was sound, whether I overlooked something or made a poor decision. And then it goes into this bigger worry about retirement, like whether I’ll become useless or sort of fade into irrelevance once I’m fully out of that role.\n\nSo it’s not random exactly, but it’s a wide range within that whole part of my life. And honestly, even to me, the amount of worry feels out of proportion. I can hear myself say it and think, “Well, that’s probably more than the situation calls for,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life right now that you feel completely confident and anxiety-free about?\n\nPatient: Completely confident and anxiety-free? No… I don’t think I can honestly say that. There are moments where I’m distracted or where I can tell myself, “This is probably okay,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an area where the worry is just absent.\n\nMy mind keeps circling back to things, even when I’ve already gone over them. I’ll think about my old patients and whether they’re really being looked after properly, whether I left things in good enough order. Then I’ll shift to the handoff of the practice — especially the financial side — and start wondering if it was sound, if I missed something, if I should have done something differently. And then it sort of broadens into retirement in general, like… what am I going to be once I’m fully done? Am I going to become useless, or irrelevant?\n\nI know that probably sounds excessive. It feels excessive to me too, honestly. But knowing that doesn’t make me feel completely confident about any of it. It just keeps looping around.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the future mostly focused on the transition to retirement or does it extend to other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly clustered around the retirement transition, yes, but I wouldn’t say it stays neatly there. That’s sort of the center of it, I suppose — stepping away from the practice, not being the person in charge anymore, wondering what my days and my purpose are going to look like once I’m fully retired.\n\nBut then it spreads into all these connected things. I keep thinking about my old patients, whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left them in good hands, whether something might be missed now that I’m not watching over it the way I used to. And then I’ll get pulled into the practice handoff itself, especially the financial side of it — whether it was really sound, whether I overlooked something, whether I should have handled some part of it differently.\n\nAnd underneath all of that is this worry that I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant, once I’m not “the dentist” anymore. I know that sounds a bit dramatic. Even as I’m saying it, part of me thinks, “This is out of proportion.” But my mind keeps circling around it anyway. So yes, retirement is the main doorway into it, but once I start worrying, it touches a lot of things around that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Completely absent? No, not really. I wish I could point to something and say, “Yes, that part of my day is untouched by it,” but I don’t think that’s true.

Even ordinary parts of the day can get pulled into it, because my mind starts circling again. I’ll be doing something routine and suddenly I’m back to thinking about my old patients — whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left everything in good order, whether there’s some detail I didn’t pass along well enough. Then it shifts to the practice handoff, especially the financial side, and I start wondering whether it was as sound as I thought, whether I overlooked something important.

And then it becomes this larger thing about retirement itself — what I’m going to be without that role, whether I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant. I know, when I say it out loud, that it sounds bigger than it probably needs to be. It feels out of proportion even to me. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a clean area of daily life where the worry just isn’t there at all. It may quiet down for a little while, but it doesn’t really disappear.

That’s sort of the center of it, I suppose — stepping away from the practice, not being the person in charge anymore, wondering what my days and my purpose are going to look like once I’m fully retired.\n\nBut then it spreads into all these connected things. I keep thinking about my old patients, whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left them in good hands, whether something might be missed now that I’m not watching over it the way I used to. And then I’ll get pulled into the practice handoff itself, especially the financial side of it — whether it was really sound, whether I overlooked something, whether I should have handled some part of it differently.\n\nAnd underneath all of that is this worry that I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant, once I’m not “the dentist” anymore. I know that sounds a bit dramatic. Even as I’m saying it, part of me thinks, “This is out of proportion.” But my mind keeps circling around it anyway. So yes, retirement is the main doorway into it, but once I start worrying, it touches a lot of things around that.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your daily life where you feel your anxiety or worry is completely absent?\n\nPatient: Completely absent? No, not really. I wish I could point to something and say, “Yes, that part of my day is untouched by it,” but I don’t think that’s true.\n\nEven ordinary parts of the day can get pulled into it, because my mind starts circling again. I’ll be doing something routine and suddenly I’m back to thinking about my old patients — whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left everything in good order, whether there’s some detail I didn’t pass along well enough. Then it shifts to the practice handoff, especially the financial side, and I start wondering whether it was as sound as I thought, whether I overlooked something important.\n\nAnd then it becomes this larger thing about retirement itself — what I’m going to be without that role, whether I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant. I know, when I say it out loud, that it sounds bigger than it probably needs to be. It feels out of proportion even to me. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a clean area of daily life where the worry just isn’t there at all. It may quiet down for a little while, but it doesn’t really disappear.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
  },

    "content": "Return the JSON now."

What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well… what finally got me to make the appointment is that my concentration has become unreliable in a way I can’t just brush off anymore. I’m a dentist, so I’m used to needing to be very precise and present, and lately I’ll be reviewing a patient chart at the clinic and suddenly realize I’ve read the same line two or three times and still haven’t really taken it in.\n\nAnd it’s not just work. I paint watercolors, and even when I’m trying to lay out a wash—something that usually settles me—I’ll drift off in my head and start thinking about everything that could go wrong. Then I come back and I’m not sure where I was, or I’ve lost the thread of what I was doing. It’s frustrating, honestly, because I’ve always prided myself on being careful and focused. Now it feels like my mind keeps wandering away from me.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling sad or down lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d call it sad, exactly. I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. But “sad or down” in the sense of feeling depressed… I don’t know, that doesn’t quite feel like the main thing.\n\nIt’s more that I get frustrated with myself, and then I start thinking too much about why I’m feeling that way, and whether it means something is slipping. Though it’s not always like a sudden panic or anything dramatic. I know that. But knowing it doesn’t make it stop. I’ve heard people talk about that kind of thing, and that doesn’t really sound like what happens to me.\n\nFor me it’s more… the worry builds and stays there, or it keeps returning, rather than hitting suddenly like a wave. That feels like a very strong word. I do worry about the future, certainly, and sometimes I get caught imagining it in a rather bleak way — like I won’t know who I am once I’m no longer working, or that I’ll have lost the part of my life that gave it structure.\n\nBut hopeless… I don’t think that’s quite right. It’s more uncertainty, and fear, and maybe some sadness around the transition. I can still imagine things being all right, at least in principle. I just don’t always feel confident about getting there.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel like you've lost interest in activities that you used to enjoy?\n\nPatient: I’m not completely sure how to answer that. I wouldn’t say I’ve lost interest in things in a total way, like they mean nothing to me now. It’s more… I don’t always get to them, or I hesitate to start them, and then I wonder whether that counts as losing interest.\n\nI still like the idea of things I’ve always enjoyed. I can still look at a garden bed and think, yes, I’d like to work on that, or see my painting things and feel some pull toward them. But sometimes the actual doing of it feels harder to settle into than it used to. So I don’t know if the interest is gone, exactly, or if it’s just crowded out.\n\nIf you mean, do I feel no pleasure or no desire for anything I used to enjoy — no, I don’t think that’s quite it. It’s just not as easy or as natural as it was.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a significant amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Not that I’ve noticed, no. I mean, I haven’t been weighing myself carefully every day or anything like that, but my clothes seem to fit about the same, and I don’t think anyone has commented that I look thinner.\n\nMy appetite can vary a bit in the ordinary way, but in terms of a significant weight loss — no, I don’t think so. If there’s been any change, it hasn’t been enough for me to be aware of it.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry usually focused on a specific situation or problem, or does it seem to be more general and unrelated to any particular thing?\n\nPatient: It’s more general, I think. I mean, it attaches itself to specific things, so if you asked me at any given moment I could probably tell you what I’m worrying about — the practice, the transition, whether my old patients are being properly cared for, whether the handoff was financially sensible, that sort of thing.\n\nBut it doesn’t feel like there’s just one problem and if that were solved, I’d be fine. My mind sort of keeps circling around all of it. One concern leads to another. It’s not like I sit down and decide, “All right, now I’m going to worry about this.” It’s more that the thoughts are just sort of there in the background, and then if I’m not fully occupied, they come forward.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around things — whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether the practice handoff was financially sound, whether I made the right decisions, whether once I’m fully retired I’ll just become sort of… irrelevant, or useless. If there’s a quiet moment, there it is again.\n\nDoctor: Are there any areas of your life where you feel completely worry-free?\n\nPatient: Completely worry-free… honestly, I’m not sure I’d say there are many areas that feel completely free of it. It’s more like the worry changes subjects depending on where my attention goes.\n\nIf I’m thinking about my old patients, I start wondering whether they’re being looked after properly, whether they feel abandoned, whether the transition was handled well enough. I keep coming back to this fear that I’ll become useless somehow, which even as I say it sounds a little excessive. I know that. Part of me can see the worry is out of proportion. I wish I could say there were, but it tends to attach itself to whatever feels important at the time.\n\nLike, if I’m thinking about my former patients, my mind starts turning over whether they’re being cared for properly, whether they feel I stepped away too abruptly, whether I did enough to make sure they were in good hands. And then if I try to reassure myself about that, it shifts to the practice handoff — whether the financial side was sound, whether I missed something, whether I made a decision that’s going to come back to haunt me somehow.\n\nAnd then there’s this larger thing about retirement itself. I keep worrying that once I’m fully retired I’ll become… I don’t know, useless, or irrelevant. Saying it out loud, I can hear that it’s probably too much. It feels out of proportion even to me. But knowing that doesn’t really make it disappear.\n\nSo I wouldn’t say the worry is completely absent anywhere. It quiets down sometimes, maybe, but it doesn’t feel gone.\n\nDoctor: Is there a particular situation or set of situations that triggers your anxiety, or does it feel more constant and unrelated to specific events?\n\nPatient: It’s a bit of both, I think. There are definitely situations that set it off more strongly — anything connected to the practice handoff, for example. If I get an email or have to look over some detail about the financial side, my mind can latch onto it and just keep going: Did I miss something? Was that arrangement really sound? Should I have handled it differently?\n\nBut it doesn’t stay neatly contained to that. It sort of spreads. I’ll start with the practice, and then I’m thinking about my old patients — whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left things in a way that was fair to them, whether someone is going to fall through the cracks because I’m not there watching over things the way I used to.\n\nAnd then it goes even wider than that, into retirement itself. I’ll catch myself worrying that once I’m fully retired, I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant. I know that sounds dramatic, and honestly it feels out of proportion even to me when I say it. My mind keeps circling around all sorts of things, and even when one concern quiets down, another one seems to take its place.\n\nDoctor: Is your anxiety usually about one or two main things, or is it about a wide range of topics?\n\nPatient: It’s definitely more than one or two things. I mean, there are a few main themes that I seem to come back to, but they branch out pretty quickly.\n\nMy mind keeps circling around whether my old patients are being looked after properly, whether I left them in good hands, whether something important is going to be missed now that I’m not the one overseeing it. Then it shifts to the practice handoff — the financial side, whether it was sound, whether I overlooked something or made a poor decision. And then it goes into this bigger worry about retirement, like whether I’ll become useless or sort of fade into irrelevance once I’m fully out of that role.\n\nSo it’s not random exactly, but it’s a wide range within that whole part of my life. And honestly, even to me, the amount of worry feels out of proportion. I can hear myself say it and think, “Well, that’s probably more than the situation calls for,” but my mind just keeps going back over it anyway.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your life right now that you feel completely confident and anxiety-free about?\n\nPatient: Completely confident and anxiety-free? No… I don’t think I can honestly say that. There are moments where I’m distracted or where I can tell myself, “This is probably okay,” but it doesn’t feel like there’s an area where the worry is just absent.\n\nMy mind keeps circling back to things, even when I’ve already gone over them. I’ll think about my old patients and whether they’re really being looked after properly, whether I left things in good enough order. Then I’ll shift to the handoff of the practice — especially the financial side — and start wondering if it was sound, if I missed something, if I should have done something differently.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It can be triggered by specific things, but honestly it also seems to start on its own. The obvious trigger is anything that reminds me I’m not really in charge of the practice anymore, or that I’m moving fully into retirement. Something as small as thinking about an old patient, or remembering a detail from the handoff, can set it off.

Then my mind starts circling. I’ll wonder whether my old patients are truly being looked after properly, whether I left enough information, whether the transition was handled the right way. From there it can jump to the financial side of the practice handoff — whether it was sound, whether I missed something, whether there’s some problem I should have caught.

And then, even if there wasn’t a clear trigger, it drifts into this bigger worry about what I’m going to be once I’m fully retired. Whether I’ll become useless, or sort of irrelevant. I know that sounds out of proportion. I can hear myself saying it and think, “Well, that’s probably more worry than the situation deserves.” But once it starts, it’s hard to get my mind to let go of it.

I mean, I have moments where I feel discouraged or kind of worn down by all of this, especially when I’m not functioning the way I expect myself to. It just keeps looping around.\n\nDoctor: Is your worry about the future mostly focused on the transition to retirement or does it extend to other areas of your life as well?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly clustered around the retirement transition, yes, but I wouldn’t say it stays neatly there. I keep thinking about my old patients, whether they’re being looked after properly, whether I left them in good hands, whether something might be missed now that I’m not watching over it the way I used to. And then I’ll get pulled into the practice handoff itself, especially the financial side of it — whether it was really sound, whether I overlooked something, whether I should have handled some part of it differently.\n\nAnd underneath all of that is this worry that I’ll become useless somehow, or irrelevant, once I’m not “the dentist” anymore. I know that sounds a bit dramatic. Even as I’m saying it, part of me thinks, “This is out of proportion.” But my mind keeps circling around it anyway. So yes, retirement is the main doorway into it, but once I start worrying, it touches a lot of things around that.\n\nDoctor: Is there anything in your daily life where you feel your anxiety or worry is completely absent?\n\nPatient: Completely absent? No, not really. I wish I could point to something and say, “Yes, that part of my day is untouched by it,” but I don’t think that’s true.\n\nEven ordinary parts of the day can get pulled into it, because my mind starts circling again. I know, when I say it out loud, that it sounds bigger than it probably needs to be. It feels out of proportion even to me. But it doesn’t feel like there’s a clean area of daily life where the worry just isn’t there at all. It may quiet down for a little while, but it doesn’t really disappear.\n\nDoctor: Is there a specific trigger or situation that usually sets off your worrying, or does it seem to happen without any particular cause?\n\nPatient: It can be triggered by specific things, but honestly it also seems to start on its own. The obvious trigger is anything that reminds me I’m not really in charge of the practice anymore, or that I’m moving fully into retirement. Something as small as thinking about an old patient, or remembering a detail from the handoff, can set it off.\n\nThen my mind starts circling. I’ll wonder whether my old patients are truly being looked after properly, whether I left enough information, whether the transition was handled the right way. From there it can jump to the financial side of the practice handoff — whether it was sound, whether I missed something, whether there’s some problem I should have caught.\n\nAnd then, even if there wasn’t a clear trigger, it drifts into this bigger worry about what I’m going to be once I’m fully retired. Whether I’ll become useless, or sort of irrelevant. I know that sounds out of proportion. I can hear myself saying it and think, “Well, that’s probably more worry than the situation deserves.” But once it starts, it’s hard to get my mind to let go of it."
